{"parentUuid":"c0434475-6324-48b7-b5f9-a35155303632","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"872714b7-1488-4cc4-8bad-5341f8c86fd3","agentId":"ac33d30f61d9f0022","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01NadeZ53WNKWgoBjWcYm3gT","type":"tool_result","content":"-- Rename the table license_keys -> account_keys to reflect the new model: credits\n-- are pooled per ACCOUNT, and a key is just the credential that unlocks its owner's\n-- wallet. This is a pure rename — no rows, columns, or data change. The `key`\n-- column and the JSON wire field stay `license_key` (installed native apps depend\n-- on them); only the Postgres identifiers move to the new name.\n\nALTER TABLE \"license_keys\" RENAME TO \"account_keys\";--> statement-breakpoint\n\n-- Indexes do not auto-rename with the table; re-key all seven.\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_stripe_session\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_stripe_session\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_polar_license_key_id\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_polar_license_key_id\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_email\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_email\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_key\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_key\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_user_id\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_user_id\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_polar_customer\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_polar_customer\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER INDEX \"idx_license_keys_stripe_customer\" RENAME TO \"idx_account_keys_stripe_customer\";--> statement-breakpoint\n\n-- Constraints keep their old names after a table rename; align them with the new\n-- table name so drizzle's snapshot matches and future diffs stay clean.\nALTER TABLE \"account_keys\" RENAME CONSTRAINT \"license_keys_pkey\" TO \"account_keys_pkey\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER TABLE \"account_keys\" RENAME CONSTRAINT \"license_keys_user_id_user_id_fk\" TO \"account_keys_user_id_user_id_fk\";--> statement-breakpoint\n\n-- The referencing FK on device_validations encodes the old table name; rename it\n-- too. (Credit tables now reference \"user\", so they're unaffected.)\nALTER TABLE \"device_validations\" RENAME CONSTRAINT \"device_validations_license_key_id_license_keys_id_fk\" TO \"device_validations_license_key_id_account_keys_id_fk\";\n=== 0011 ===\n-- Re-key the credit ledger from license_key_id (per-key) to user_id (per-account).\n-- Credits become ONE pooled balance per account; a license/account key is just a\n-- credential that unlocks its owner's wallet. This migration is DATA-PRESERVING:\n-- existing per-key grants are backfilled to their owning user, and credit_balances\n-- (only a denormalized cache) is rebuilt as one row per account = SUM of that\n-- account's active, unexpired remaining_amount. No one loses credits.\n\n-- ============================================================================\n-- credit_grants: add user_id, backfill from each grant's owning license, make it\n-- NOT NULL + FK -> user, then re-key the indexes and drop license_key_id.\n-- ============================================================================\nALTER TABLE \"credit_grants\" ADD COLUMN \"user_id\" text;--> statement-breakpoint\n\n-- Backfill every grant's account from its license key. The old FK (onDelete\n-- cascade) guaranteed every grant had a live license, and every license has a\n-- non-null user_id, so this populates every row.\nUPDATE \"credit_grants\" g\nSET \"user_id\" = lk.\"user_id\"\nFROM \"license_keys\" lk\nWHERE g.\"license_key_id\" = lk.\"id\";--> statement-breakpoint\n\nALTER TABLE \"credit_grants\" ALTER COLUMN \"user_id\" SET NOT NULL;--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER TABLE \"credit_grants\" ADD CONSTRAINT \"credit_grants_user_id_user_id_fk\" FOREIGN KEY (\"user_id\") REFERENCES \"public\".\"user\"(\"id\") ON DELETE cascade ON UPDATE no action;--> statement-breakpoint\n\nDROP INDEX \"credit_grants_license_source_remaining_idx\";--> statement-breakpoint\nDROP INDEX \"credit_grants_license_status_expires_idx\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER TABLE \"credit_grants\" DROP CONSTRAINT \"credit_grants_license_key_id_license_keys_id_fk\";--> statement-breakpoint\nALTER TABLE \"credit_grants\" DROP COLUMN \"license_key_id\";--> statement-breakpoint\n\nCREATE INDEX \"credit_grants_user_source_remaining_idx\" ON \"credit_grants\" USING btree (\"user_id\",\"source_type\",\"remaining_amount\");--> statement-breakpoint\n=== tsx dep ===\n8:    \"vercel-build\": \"npx drizzle-kit migrate && npm run prebuild && next build\",\n16:    \"db:generate\": \"drizzle-kit generate\",\n17:    \"db:migrate\": \"tsx src/db/migrate.ts\"\n71:    \"drizzle-kit\": \"^0.31.9\",\n87:    \"tsx\": \"^4.21.0\",","is_error":false}]},"uuid":"1f29e073-e415-4862-935d-3ecf5a789e04","timestamp":"2026-07-29T11:48:09.101Z","toolUseResult":{"stdout":"-- Rename the table license_keys -> account_keys to reflect the new model: credits\n-- are pooled per ACCOUNT, and a key is just the credential that unlocks its owner's\n-- wallet. {"parentUuid":"e5cb7bd7-3878-4298-b253-8bbe1042ac21","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ac33d30f61d9f0022","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CdWJA7SxuEyyCbpe9vr4q","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"I traced T15. Findings below, honest about what's inert.\n\n---\n\n# Candidate bugs\n\n## F1 — Migrations commit to production Postgres before the new code exists, and two build steps that can fail run *after* them\n**confidence: high**\n\n**trace**\n1. `nextjs/vercel.json:5` — `\"buildCommand\": \"npm run db:migrate && npm run validate-appcast && npm run build\"`\n2. `nextjs/package.json:17` — `db:migrate` → `tsx src/db/migrate.ts`\n3. `nextjs/src/db/migrate.ts:6-9` — `new Pool({ connectionString: process.env.PLANETSCALE_DATABASE_URL! })` → `migrate(db, { migrationsFolder: \"./drizzle\" })` against the **production** DB\n4. `nextjs/drizzle/0012_rename_license_keys_to_account_keys.sql:7` — `ALTER TABLE \"license_keys\" RENAME TO \"account_keys\"`; `nextjs/drizzle/0011_account_credit_wallet.sql:29` — `ALTER TABLE \"credit_grants\" DROP COLUMN \"license_key_id\"`\n5. Meanwhile the **currently-aliased** deployment keeps serving `nextjs/app/api/license/validate/route.ts:57` and `nextjs/app/api/license/activate/route.ts:25`, whose Drizzle queries still name the old relation.\n\n**seam** — hop 3 (`migrate.ts`, which assumes \"I am the deploy\") ↔ hop 5 (the live Vercel deployment, which is the *previous* build's code). Vercel's order is build → deploy → alias; `buildCommand` therefore mutates prod schema minutes before the code that matches it goes live. Nothing in `migrate.ts`, `drizzle.config.ts`, or `vercel.json` encodes an expand/contract discipline, and 0011/0012 are both pure contract-phase (DROP COLUMN, RENAME TABLE).\n\n**trigger_sketch** — Push the commit containing `0012`. From the moment `migrate()` commits until the alias flips, any desktop app calling `POST /api/license/validate`, plus the Stripe webhook and the cloud backend's license lookups, hit `relation \"license_keys\" does not exist`.\n\nWorse second-order trigger: the chain is `db:migrate && validate-appcast && build`. The migration is committed **first**, then `nextjs/scripts/validate-appcast.js` (which `process.exit(1)`s on ~8 different content conditions — bad enclosure host at :48, duplicate build numbers at :85, non-descending builds at :109) and then `next build` run. Break the appcast XML or a TS type in the same commit as a migration and you get: schema migrated, build red, **no rollback of the DDL**, old code serving against the new schema *indefinitely* until a human fixes it. There is no `migrate().catch()` and no down-migration anywhere in `nextjs/drizzle/`.\n\n**consequence** — Every HyperWhisper desktop client's license validation 500s for the duration; on the failed-build path, until manual intervention. Paying users see \"license could not be verified\".\n\n**note on the `process.exit(0)` question you flagged**: `main()` at `nextjs/src/db/migrate.ts:13` is called with no `.catch()`, so a rejected `migrate()` becomes an unhandled rejection. On Node ≥15 the default is `--unhandled-rejections=throw`, so the process *does* exit non-zero and `&&` halts the build. **A failed migration does not ship as green** — that specific worry is unfounded. The real problem is the opposite: a *successful* migration followed by a failed later step.\n\n---\n\n## F2 — Cloud Redis uses different env-var names than Next, is validated nowhere, fails open, and the smoke test structurally cannot see it\n**confidence: medium-high**\n\n**trace**\n1. `nextjs/lib/clients/redis.ts:4-5` — `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL` / `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_TOKEN`, both non-null-asserted\n2. `nextjs/src/env/schema.mjs:20-21` — those two names **are** in the zod server schema (`.url()` + `.string()`), and `nextjs/next.config.mjs:8` imports `src/env/server.mjs` unless `SKIP_ENV_VALIDATION`. `SKIP_ENV_VALIDATION=1` is set only on `dev` (`package.json:6`), not `build`. So the Next side genuinely fails the build if these are missing. Good.\n3. `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/lib/redis.ts:13-14` — **different names**: `UPSTASH_REDIS_URL` / `UPSTASH_REDIS_TOKEN`. These appear in no schema, no `fly.*.toml`, and no boot check.\n4. `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/lib/redis.ts:16-18` — the missing-env `throw` is inside `getRedis()`, called lazily\n5. `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/lib/redis.ts:36-41` — `isIPBlocked()` wraps it in `try { … } catch { return false }`\n\n**seam** — hop 3/4 (lazy throw, zero boot validation, name divergence from the Next side that shares the same store) ↔ hop 5 (a blanket `catch` that converts \"Redis is not configured at all\" into \"this IP is not blocked\"). The catch was written for transient Upstash flakiness; it also swallows permanent misconfiguration.\n\n**trigger_sketch** — `fly secrets set` the Upstash values using the *Next* names (`UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL`), or simply forget them on a new Fly app. `getRedis()` throws on every call.\n\n**consequence** — IP blocking is silently and completely disabled across `/transcribe` (`routes/transcribe.ts:42`), `/post-process` (`routes/post-process.ts:10`), `/assistant`, `/usage`, `/ws-streaming-deepgram`; the license cache (`getCachedLicense`, `:53-67`) is permanently cold so every request round-trips to the Next license API; and `getGoogleAccessToken` (`hyperwhisper-cloud/src/lib/google-auth.ts:127`) re-mints a JWT on every google-chirp request. All of it silent — the only signal is `console.warn` lines.\n\n**and the deploy gate can't catch it**: `hyperwhisper-cloud/scripts/smoke-test.ts:170` asserts `/health`, which at `hyperwhisper-cloud/src/index.ts:29-35` returns a static `{status, region, timestamp}` touching nothing. `/warmup` returns a bare 204. The `/transcribe` checks still return 200 because every Redis failure is swallowed. So a fully Redis-less deployment passes staging smoke and is promoted to prod as green.\n\n---\n\n## F3 — The license rate limiter fails *closed* on a Redis outage, with no try/catch, as the first statement of the two hottest public endpoints\n**confidence: medium**\n\n**trace**\n1. `nextjs/lib/rate-limit.ts:2` — `import redis from \"@/lib/clients/redis\"` (module-level singleton, `!`-asserted)\n2. `nextjs/lib/rate-limit.ts:27-32` — `licenseValidateRateLimiter` constructed at module load; `analytics: true`, **no `timeout` and no `ephemeralCache` option**\n3. `nextjs/app/api/license/validate/route.ts:62` — `const { success } = await licenseValidateRateLimiter.limit(clientIP);` is the **first** statement of `POST`, outside any `try`\n4. Same at `nextjs/app/api/license/activate/route.ts:30`\n\n**seam** — hop 2 (a limiter whose stated job at `lib/rate-limit.ts:16-25` is purely \"cap Polar amplification abuse\") ↔ hop 3 (a route that treats its result as infallible). The limiter silently promoted Upstash from an optimization to a hard availability dependency of license validation.\n\n**trigger_sketch** — Upstash outage, token rotation, or the Upstash plan's daily command cap being hit (`analytics: true` roughly doubles commands per check). `.limit()` rejects; nothing catches it; Next returns 500. With no `timeout` set, a *hung* Upstash instead blocks until the Vercel function limit → 504.\n\n**consequence** — Every desktop client's license validate/activate 500s for the outage duration. Only `url.search` is attacker-influenced; the pathname is the literal `\"/credits\"`, and the WHATWG `URL` setter percent-encodes CR/LF and strips tabs/newlines, so no `Location` splitting. The host does come from `request.url` (Host-derived) on Vercel, but Vercel only routes hosts attached to the project to the deployment, and the response carries no cache headers, so there is no poisoning target. Real behaviour is a cosmetic double-hop (`/credits` → intl middleware → `/en/credits`). Only live issue is F5's dropped `code`.\n- **EP-57** `nextjs/app/[locale]/checkout/page.tsx` — `params.locale` cannot be hostile. `middleware.ts:150` matches the path, `src/i18n/routing.ts` uses `localePrefix: \"always\"` over the 40-entry allowlist in `src/i18n/locales.ts`, so next-intl redirects any unknown prefix before the page renders; `locale` can never contain `/` or produce `//evil.com`. `id`/`license_key`/`code` go through `URLSearchParams.set` (encoded).
